fresh frame with incremented count), sets `vm.ip = tryIP`, and continues. The retry count lives in the frame; when we pop on retry, we need to push a
new frame with count+1 (and the same saved depths, catchIP, etc.). If count >= 3 (limit), raise "retry exhausted" error which should be caught by an
outer try or propagate. Actually the task says "automatic limit of three retries before raising a distinct exhaustion error." So after 3 retries, the
4th attempt? Let's interpret: "retry - re-executes the try body; automatic limit of three retries before raising a distinct exhaustion error." I
interpret: you can call retry at most 3 times; the 4th retry raises exhaustion error. Or: after 3 retries total (i.e., the try body executed up to 3
times), if it still fails and retry is called again, raise exhaustion. I'll implement: each retry increments count; if count > 3 before re-executing,
panic with exhaustion error. So try body can execute up to 4 times (initial + 3 retries). That seems reasonable: "automatic limit of three retries"
means up to three retries allowed.

Wait, but which errors does retry apply to? If retry is called in the catch handler, it means "re-run the try body regardless of what error was
caught". So on retry, we restore and re-run. If the re-run succeeds, result is returned. If it fails again, catch runs again; if it calls retry again,
count increments. If count exceeds limit, raise exhaustion error (which would be caught by an outer try or propagate).

want `catch` to receive the thrown custom error, and `errtype` to return "custom". Let me think.

`throw(value)`:
- The message is the string conversion of value.

```go
                if len(args) != 1 {
                        return nil, fmt.Errorf("invalid number of arguments (expected 1, got %d)", len(args))
                }
```

When a `Func` returns an error, the VM panics with that error (OpCall1 etc. do `panic(err)`). So `throw` would work naturally! The compiler calls it
via `emitFunction`. It returns `(nil, error)` which causes panic with the error. That error is a plain `errors.New` — classified as "custom".

When the inner try catches, it sets inCatch=true for the inner catch. OpRetry inside inner catch should retry inner try. How does it know which try to

caught by this frame. `OpRetry` finds the innermost frame marked "in catch" and retries it.

So:
- `OpTry` pushes frame with state, `inCatch=false`.
- During try body, panics are checked against frames with `inCatch=false` (innermost such frame). If found: mark it `inCatch=true`, restore state,
jump to catch.
- During catch body, `OpRetry` finds innermost frame with `inCatch=true`, resets it to `inCatch=false`, increments count, restores state, jumps to
tryIP.
- `OpEndTry` (on success path) pops the frame.
- `OpEndCatch` (at end of catch body) pops the frame.

But there's a complication: if catch body itself throws, we need it to NOT be caught by the same frame (inCatch=true means skip), but it SHOULD be
caught by an outer try frame (inCatch=false). So the panic handler iterates frames from innermost to outermost, skipping frames with inCatch=true
until it finds one with inCatch=false. Good. If found, that outer frame's saved stack/scopes/variables are restored — which discards the inner frame
entirely (and the inner catch's state). We need to pop frames up to that point. Good.

Now `OpEndTry` on success pops the frame. `OpEndCatch` on catch-completion pops the frame. But what about the `finally` clause? The finally runs after
either success or catch. At that point the try frame should be popped (done). If finally throws, it should propagate to outer try (not be caught by
this try). Good — frame already popped before finally.

retryCount remains. If retryCount >= limit when OpRetry executes, it should raise exhaustion error instead of retrying. But OpRetry is executing
inside catch; the frame has inCatch=true. Raising an error inside catch should propagate to outer try (not be caught by this same try, since

Good.

Now, the stack/scopes/variables restore. When a try body panics, partial values on the stack need to be discarded, and scopes begun inside try need to
be unwound. We save `stackDepth = len(vm.Stack)` at OpTry and `scopesDepth = len(vm.Scopes)`. On error/retry, truncate. For variables: if try body
declares `let x = ...` and then throws, x remains set. On retry, we want to reset? Upstream probably snapshots variables. But variables array has a
fixed size for the whole program; declaring `let x` inside try writes to `vm.Variables[idx]`. To be safe, snapshot the Variables slice on OpTry and
restore on error/retry. But the Variables slice is shared and grows; we can copy at OpTry. However, OpTry happens at runtime; copying `vm.Variables`
(len up to program.variables) each try is fine.

Wait, but there's a subtlety: variable declaration inside try may call `addVariable` at compile time, which increases `program.variables`. The
snapshot copies the current values. On error, restore. Good.

Now, memory (`vm.memory`) and `fnArgsBuf` are not restored. `fnArgsBuf` is a local in Run; on error, its position might have advanced. Since retry
re-executes from try start, the buffer pointer would still be advanced and might overflow into new allocations — that's fine (getArgsForFunc handles).
Memory budget grows; retries don't reset memory. Probably fine.

Now, how to implement the per-opcode recover wrapper. Current Run has a big `switch op` inline. Refactoring the whole switch into a method would be a
big change but cleanest. Alternatively, we can wrap only the loop body execution with a helper closure that contains a switch. Let me refactor the
switch into a method `func (vm *VM) execute(program *Program, env any, op Opcode, arg int, fnArgsBuf *[]any)`? But `fnArgsBuf` is a local and needs to
be shared. Actually `fnArgsBuf` is used by OpCall1/2/3/N/Fast/Safe and passed by value and returned. Currently the loop does `args, fnArgsBuf =
vm.getArgsForFunc(fnArgsBuf, program, 1)`. If we move to a method, fnArgsBuf would need to be a VM field or passed as pointer.

Simplest: add `fnArgsBuf []any` as a VM field (reset each Run). But VM struct is exported and has fields; adding is fine. Or pass `*[]any` to execute
method.

Actually, maybe a simpler approach: wrap the entire `for` loop iteration in a function using a closure defined inside Run. But the switch references
`program`, `env`, `fnArgsBuf`, `debug`. We can keep the loop inline and only wrap the panic-catchable operations. But errors can occur in any opcode
(e.g., OpAdd panics via runtime.Add). So we need to wrap all opcodes.

Let me refactor: move the big switch into a method on VM:
```go
func (vm *VM) execute(program *Program, env any, op Opcode, arg int, fnArgsBuf *[]any)
```
And in Run:
```go
for vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode) {
        ...
        op := ...
        arg := ...
        vm.ip += 1

        if debug && vm.debug { <-vm.step }

        if len(vm.tries) > 0 {
                func() {
                        defer func() {
                                if r := recover(); r != nil {
                                        vm.handlePanic(program, r)
                                }
                        }()
                        vm.execute(program, env, op, arg, &fnArgsBuf)
                }()
        } else {
                vm.execute(program, env, op, arg, &fnArgsBuf)
        }

        if debug && vm.debug { vm.curr <- vm.ip }
}
```

But wait — there's a subtlety: if `vm.execute` panics and `handlePanic` handles it, we're inside the deferred function. After `handlePanic` returns,
the closure returns, then the loop continues. Good. If `handlePanic` re-panics (no matching frame), the outer top-level defer catches it and converts
to *file.Error. Good.

But there's an issue: `fnArgsBuf` as a local variable — after a panic inside OpCall1, the `args` slice may have been taken from the buffer but the
buffer advancement (`argsBuf = argsBuf[needed:]`) happened inside `getArgsForFunc` before the call. If the call panics and we restore the stack, the
buffer pointer is advanced. On retry, `getArgsForFunc` will continue from there; it handles `len(argsBuf) < needed` by allocating. So functionally
fine, just may allocate more. Acceptable.

Also `vm.memory` — not restored. Fine.

Now, the `handlePanic` needs the error value. The panic value could be:
- an `error` (e.g., from OpThrow with error, or function calls returning error, or custom throw via errors.New)
- a string (e.g., runtime panics like "index out of range", or panic(fmt.Sprintf(...)) where fmt.Sprintf returns string)
- other.

We need to convert to a catchable error value. For catch variable, the user gets... what? Probably the error as an `error` or `*file.Error`.
`errtype(err)` should classify it. For uncaught, the top-level recover wraps into `*file.Error`.

Let's define a helper to convert panic value to an error:
```go
func runtimeError(r any) error {
        if e, ok := r.(error); ok {
                return e
        }
        return errors.New(fmt.Sprintf("%v", r))
}
```

But for classification, we need to know the error "type". Since most runtime panics are strings, and custom throws are `errors.New`, we can classify
based on the message string. The task says:
- `"index"` for out-of-range/bounds errors
- `"conversion"` for type-conversion failures
- `"type"` for type-mismatch/assertion errors
- `"nil"` for nil-pointer/reference errors
- `"retry"` for retry-exhaustion errors
- `"custom"` for all other errors including those from `throw`
- `"none"` when the input is nil

So `errtype` receives an error value and inspects its message (string conversion) with substring matching. Let me define categories by message
patterns:
- index: messages containing "index out of range", "out of range", "bounds out of range", "slice bounds out of range", etc.
- conversion: messages containing "cannot convert", "invalid operation: int(", "strconv", "conversion", "cannot parse", "invalid syntax", etc.
- type: messages containing "type assertion", "cannot use", "mismatched type", "cannot call", "is not callable", "cannot fetch", "invalid operation:
... ", "type"...
- nil: messages containing "nil", "nil pointer", "dereference", "invalid memory address", "cannot get", "cannot fetch" ... hmm.
- retry: retry exhaustion.
- custom: everything else.

This is inherently fuzzy. Let me look at the actual runtime messages in this codebase:
- Index out of range: runtime.go `panic(fmt.Sprintf("index out of range: %v (array length is %v)", index, l))`, `panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot fetch %v
from %T", i, from))` (Fetch on array out of range? let's check), `reflect: slice index out of range` (OpThrow), runtime panics from Go itself like
"reflect: slice index out of range", "runtime error: index out of range".
- Conversion: runtime.ToInt etc: `panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: int(%T)", x))` etc.
- Type mismatch: runtime helpers `panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: %T + %T", a, b))`, `cannot fetch %v from %T`, `cannot get %v from %T`, `cannot
slice %T`, `operator "in" not defined on %T`, `invalid argument for len (type %T)`, `invalid operation: cannot call nil`, `cannot call non-function of
type %T`, type assertion failures in `vm.pop().(int)` etc. (those panic with Go's "interface conversion: ..." message).
- Nil: `runtime.Fetch` on nil? Let's check runtime.go. Also deref of nil pointer can panic with Go's "runtime error: invalid memory address or nil
pointer dereference".

Let me read vm/runtime.go fully to know the messages.

package runtime

//go:generate sh -c "go run ./helpers > ./helpers[generated].go"

import (
	"fmt"
	"math"
	"reflect"
	"sync"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/internal/deref"
)

var fieldCache sync.Map

type fieldCacheKey struct {
	t reflect.Type
	f string
}

func Fetch(from, i any) any {
	v := reflect.ValueOf(from)
	if v.Kind() == reflect.Invalid {
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot fetch %v from %T", i, from))
	}

	// Methods can be defined on any type.
	if v.NumMethod() > 0 {
		if methodName, ok := i.(string); ok {
			method := v.MethodByName(methodName)
			if method.IsValid() {
				return method.Interface()
			}
		}
	}

	// Structs, maps, and slices can be access through a pointer or through
	// a value, when they are accessed through a pointer we don't want to
	// copy them to a value.
	// De-reference everything if necessary (interface and pointers)
	v = deref.Value(v)

	switch v.Kind() {
	case reflect.Array, reflect.Slice, reflect.String:
		index := ToInt(i)
		l := v.Len()
		if index < 0 {
			index = l + index
		}
		if index < 0 || index >= l {
			panic(fmt.Sprintf("index out of range: %v (array length is %v)", index, l))
		}
		value := v.Index(index)
		if value.IsValid() {
			return value.Interface()
		}

	case reflect.Map:
		var value reflect.Value
		if i == nil {
			value = v.MapIndex(reflect.Zero(v.Type().Key()))
		} else {
			value = v.MapIndex(reflect.ValueOf(i))
		}
		if value.IsValid() {
			return value.Interface()
		} else {
			elem := reflect.TypeOf(from).Elem()
			return reflect.Zero(elem).Interface()
		}

	case reflect.Struct:
		fieldName := i.(string)
		t := v.Type()
		key := fieldCacheKey{
			t: t,
			f: fieldName,
		}
		if cv, ok := fieldCache.Load(key); ok {
			return v.FieldByIndex(cv.([]int)).Interface()
		}
		field, ok := t.FieldByNameFunc(func(name string) bool {
			field, _ := t.FieldByName(name)
			switch field.Tag.Get("expr") {
			case "-":
				return false
			case fieldName:
				return true
			default:
				return name == fieldName
			}
		})
		if ok && field.IsExported() {
			value := v.FieldByIndex(field.Index)
			if value.IsValid() {
				fieldCache.Store(key, field.Index)
				return value.Interface()
			}
		}
	}
	panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot fetch %v from %T", i, from))
}

type Field struct {
	Index []int
	Path  []string
}

func FetchField(from any, field *Field) any {
	v := reflect.ValueOf(from)
	if v.Kind() != reflect.Invalid {
		v = reflect.Indirect(v)

		// We can use v.FieldByIndex here, but it will panic if the field
		// is not exists. And we need to recover() to generate a more
		// user-friendly error message.
		// Also, our fieldByIndex() function is slightly faster than the
		// v.FieldByIndex() function as we don't need to verify what a field
		// is a struct as we already did it on compilation step.
		value := fieldByIndex(v, field)
		if value.IsValid() {
			return value.Interface()
		}
	}
	panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot get %v from %T", field.Path[0], from))
}

func fieldByIndex(v reflect.Value, field *Field) reflect.Value {
	if len(field.Index) == 1 {
		return v.Field(field.Index[0])
	}
	for i, x := range field.Index {
		if i > 0 {
			if v.Kind() == reflect.Ptr {
				if v.IsNil() {
					panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot get %v from %v", field.Path[i], field.Path[i-1]))
				}
				v = v.Elem()
			}
		}
		v = v.Field(x)
	}
	return v
}

type Method struct {
	Index int
	Name  string
}

func FetchMethod(from any, method *Method) any {
	v := reflect.ValueOf(from)
	kind := v.Kind()
	if kind != reflect.Invalid {
		// Methods can be defined on any type, no need to dereference.
		method := v.Method(method.Index)
		if method.IsValid() {
			return method.Interface()
		}
	}
	panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot fetch %v from %T", method.Name, from))
}

func Slice(array, from, to any) any {
	v := reflect.ValueOf(array)

	switch v.Kind() {
	case reflect.Array, reflect.Slice, reflect.String:
		length := v.Len()
		a, b := ToInt(from), ToInt(to)
		if a < 0 {
			a = length + a
		}
		if a < 0 {
			a = 0
		}
		if b < 0 {
			b = length + b
		}
		if b < 0 {
			b = 0
		}
		if b > length {
			b = length
		}
		if a > b {
			a = b
		}
		if v.Kind() == reflect.Array && !v.CanAddr() {
			newValue := reflect.New(v.Type()).Elem()
			newValue.Set(v)
			v = newValue
		}
		value := v.Slice(a, b)
		if value.IsValid() {
			return value.Interface()
		}

	case reflect.Ptr:
		value := v.Elem()
		if value.IsValid() {
			return Slice(value.Interface(), from, to)
		}

	}
	panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot slice %v", from))
}

func In(needle any, array any) bool {
	if array == nil {
		return false
	}
	v := reflect.ValueOf(array)

	switch v.Kind() {

	case reflect.Array, reflect.Slice:
		for i := 0; i < v.Len(); i++ {
			value := v.Index(i)
			if value.IsValid() {
				if Equal(value.Interface(), needle) {
					return true
				}
			}
		}
		return false

	case reflect.Map:
		var value reflect.Value
		if needle == nil {
			value = v.MapIndex(reflect.Zero(v.Type().Key()))
		} else {
			value = v.MapIndex(reflect.ValueOf(needle))
		}
		if value.IsValid() {
			return true
		}
		return false

	case reflect.Struct:
		n := reflect.ValueOf(needle)
		if !n.IsValid() || n.Kind() != reflect.String {
			panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot use %T as field name of %T", needle, array))
		}
		field, ok := v.Type().FieldByName(n.String())
		if !ok || !field.IsExported() || field.Tag.Get("expr") == "-" {
			return false
		}
		value := v.FieldByIndex(field.Index)
		if value.IsValid() {
			return true
		}
		return false

	case reflect.Ptr:
		value := v.Elem()
		if value.IsValid() {
			return In(needle, value.Interface())
		}
		return false
	}

	panic(fmt.Sprintf(`operator "in" not defined on %T`, array))
}

func Len(a any) int {
	v := reflect.ValueOf(a)
	switch v.Kind() {
	case reflect.Array, reflect.Slice, reflect.Map, reflect.String:
		return v.Len()
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid argument for len (type %T)", a))
	}
}

func Negate(i any) any {
	switch v := i.(type) {
	case float32:
		return -v
	case float64:
		return -v
	case int:
		return -v
	case int8:
		return -v
	case int16:
		return -v
	case int32:
		return -v
	case int64:
		return -v
	case uint:
		return -v
	case uint8:
		return -v
	case uint16:
		return -v
	case uint32:
		return -v
	case uint64:
		return -v
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: - %T", v))
	}
}

func Exponent(a, b any) float64 {
	return math.Pow(ToFloat64(a), ToFloat64(b))
}

func MakeRange(min, max int) []int {
	size := max - min + 1
	if size <= 0 {
		return []int{}
	}
	rng := make([]int, size)
	for i := range rng {
		rng[i] = min + i
	}
	return rng
}

func ToInt(a any) int {
	switch x := a.(type) {
	case float32:
		return int(x)
	case float64:
		return int(x)
	case int:
		return x
	case int8:
		return int(x)
	case int16:
		return int(x)
	case int32:
		return int(x)
	case int64:
		return int(x)
	case uint:
		return int(x)
	case uint8:
		return int(x)
	case uint16:
		return int(x)
	case uint32:
		return int(x)
	case uint64:
		return int(x)
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: int(%T)", x))
	}
}

func ToInt64(a any) int64 {
	switch x := a.(type) {
	case float32:
		return int64(x)
	case float64:
		return int64(x)
	case int:
		return int64(x)
	case int8:
		return int64(x)
	case int16:
		return int64(x)
	case int32:
		return int64(x)
	case int64:
		return x
	case uint:
		return int64(x)
	case uint8:
		return int64(x)
	case uint16:
		return int64(x)
	case uint32:
		return int64(x)
	case uint64:
		return int64(x)
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: int64(%T)", x))
	}
}

func ToFloat64(a any) float64 {
	switch x := a.(type) {
	case float32:
		return float64(x)
	case float64:
		return x
	case int:
		return float64(x)
	case int8:

	case uint16:
		return float64(x)
	case uint32:
		return float64(x)
	case uint64:
		return float64(x)
	default:

	}
}

func ToBool(a any) bool {
	if a == nil {
		return false
	}
	switch x := a.(type) {
	case bool:
		return x
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: bool(%T)", x))
	}
}

func IsNil(v any) bool {
	if v == nil {
		return true
	}
	r := reflect.ValueOf(v)
	switch r.Kind() {
	case reflect.Chan, reflect.Func, reflect.Map, reflect.Ptr, reflect.Interface, reflect.Slice:
		return r.IsNil()
	default:
		return false
	}
}

matches. I'll classify by message patterns; custom throw with "index out of range" would be "index". That's acceptable given the spec's wording.

Actually, to make `throw` reliably "custom", I could wrap thrown errors in a custom error type with a marker. Then `errtype` checks marker first →
"custom". And OpThrow/OpCall panics with this marker type. But runtime panics from builtins that return errors (e.g., aggregate functions returning

```go
type RuntimeError struct {
        Message string
}
func (e *RuntimeError) Error() string { return e.Message }
```

Now, `errtype` classification needs to handle Go runtime panics like "runtime error: index out of range" (from slicing arrays with reflect), "runtime
error: invalid memory address or nil pointer dereference", "interface conversion: interface {} is int, not string". These come from Go's own panics
when the VM does type assertions. Our per-opcode recover catches them as panic values (runtime.Error). Their `.Error()` strings:
- "runtime error: index out of range [3] with length 2"
- "runtime error: invalid memory address or nil pointer dereference"
- "interface conversion: interface {} is int, not string"

Classification patterns:
- index: contains "index out of range", "out of range", "slice bounds out of range", "array bounds", "cannot fetch ..." hmm. Let's use "out of range"
as key.
- conversion: contains "conversion", "cannot convert", "invalid operation: int(", "invalid operation: float(", "strconv", "invalid syntax", "cannot
parse".
- type: contains "interface conversion", "type assertion", "cannot use", "mismatched type", "cannot call", "is not callable", "not defined on",
"invalid argument for", "invalid operation: ", "cannot fetch", "cannot get", "cannot slice", "no field", "no method".
- nil: contains "nil pointer", "nil", "invalid memory address", "cannot get" hmm but "cannot get" is type/field error. Let's use "nil pointer
dereference", "nil map", "cannot dereference", "is nil". But "is nil; cannot call nil as function" — that's more type/nil. Task says "nil" for
nil-pointer/reference errors. I'll use: "nil pointer", "invalid memory address", "nil map", "nil function", "cannot call nil", "is nil".
- retry: contains "retry" / "retry exhausted".
- custom: otherwise.

Let me write it:
```go
func ErrType(arg any) any {
        if arg == nil {
                return "none"
        }
        var msg string
        switch v := arg.(type) {
        case error:
                msg = v.Error()
        case string:
                msg = v
        default:
                msg = fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)
        }
        switch {
        case strings.Contains(msg, "out of range"), strings.Contains(msg, "out-of-range"), strings.Contains(msg, "bounds out of range"):
                return "index"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "conversion"), strings.Contains(msg, "cannot convert"), strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation: int("),
strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation: int64("), strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation: float("), strings.Contains(msg, "strconv."),
strings.Contains(msg, "invalid syntax"):
                return "conversion"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "nil pointer"), strings.Contains(msg, "invalid memory address"), strings.Contains(msg, "is nil"),
strings.Contains(msg, "cannot call nil"), strings.Contains(msg, "nil map"):
                return "nil"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "interface conversion"), strings.Contains(msg, "type assertion"), strings.Contains(msg, "cannot use"),
strings.Contains(msg, "mismatched type"), strings.Contains(msg, "is not callable"), strings.Contains(msg, "cannot call non-function"),
strings.Contains(msg, "not defined on"), strings.Contains(msg, "invalid argument for"), strings.Contains(msg, "cannot fetch"), strings.Contains(msg,
"cannot get"), strings.Contains(msg, "cannot slice"), strings.Contains(msg, "no field"), strings.Contains(msg, "no method"), strings.Contains(msg,
"invalid operation:"):
                return "type"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "retry exhausted"), strings.Contains(msg, "retry limit"):
                return "retry"
        default:
                return "custom"
        }
}
```

Order matters: `invalid operation: int(string)` contains "invalid operation:" so should be conversion; put conversion before type. `invalid operation:
cannot call nil` contains "cannot call nil" → nil; but also "invalid operation:" → type if we check type first. We put nil before type. Good.

Now, `retry` exhaustion: we need a distinct error. Define `ErrRetryExhausted = errors.New("retry limit exceeded")` perhaps in vm/runtime. When OpRetry
hits limit, panic with that. `errtype` returns "retry".

Now let's think about the checker for `throw`, `retry`, `errtype`, and `try`.

`throw(value)`:
- Builtin with `Func` that returns error.
- Checker: Validate requires exactly 1 arg; return any (or maybe no return since it never returns normally, but type system doesn't have never).
Return any.
- Compiler: it's a normal builtin with Func, so compiles via emitFunction → OpCall1. At runtime panics. Good.

`retry()`:
- Builtin with no Func? If we register it as a builtin, the parser will create a BuiltinNode. The compiler must special-case `retry` to emit `OpRetry`
instead of calling a function. The checker must require exactly 0 args and return any (or never).
- Actually, should retry be a builtin function call `retry()` or a keyword? The task says "`retry` - usable inside catch blocks" and "Using retry
outside a catch block raises a runtime error." The syntax `retry()` is a function call. I'll implement as builtin `retry` requiring 0 args. The
compiler special-cases name "retry" to emit OpRetry.
- But wait, if `retry` is a builtin, users could also use it as an identifier? builtin.Index would make parser treat `retry` followed by `(` as
BuiltinNode. If used as bare identifier `retry`, it becomes IdentifierNode and would be unknown unless env has it. That's fine.
- The checker should require exactly 0 args for `retry`.

`errtype(err)`:
- Builtin with `Fast` function? It takes one arg and returns string. Could use `Fast: ErrType` (1 arg). But Fast functions are called with
`OpCallBuiltin1` which expects exactly 1 arg (the compiler emits OpCallBuiltin1 for Fast builtins regardless of arg count? Let's check:
`c.emit(OpCallBuiltin1, id)` for any arg count — but Fast builtins are only used with 1 arg in practice. For errtype, exactly 1 arg is required, so
Fast is fine. But if someone calls `errtype(a, b)`, checker Validate should reject. Also compiler emits OpCallBuiltin1 with one popped arg; if there
were 2 args it would break. Since checker rejects, fine. Let's set `Fast: ErrType` and `Validate` requiring 1 arg, `Types: func(any) string`.
- The builtin's Fast gets the raw value (error or nil). Good.

Now `try`:
- Parser creates `TryNode`. Checker and compiler handle it specially.

### Checker implementation for TryNode

```go
func (v *Checker) tryNode(node *ast.TryNode) Nature {
        tryNT := v.visit(node.Try)
        var catchNT Nature
        if node.Catch != nil {
                if node.Name != "" {
                        v.varScopes = append(v.varScopes, varScope{name: node.Name, nature: v.config.NtCache.NatureOf((error)(nil))})
                }
                catchNT = v.visit(node.Catch)
                if node.Name != "" {
                        v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]
                }
        }
        var finallyNT Nature
        if node.Finally != nil {
                finallyNT = v.visit(node.Finally)
        }
        _ = finallyNT
        // join try and catch types
        if catchNT.Type == nil || catchNT.Type == anyType {
                return tryNT
        }
        if tryNT.Type == nil || tryNT.Type == anyType {
                return catchNT
        }
        if tryNT.Type == catchNT.Type {
                return tryNT
        }
        return v.config.NtCache.NatureOf(anyType?) // hmm
}
```

Actually `Nature` has methods. Let me look at how to represent any type. `v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)` gives any. Let me return that. But also
there's the Nil flags etc. Let's just:
```go
if tryNT.Type == catchNT.Type && tryNT.Type != nil {
        return tryNT
}
return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
```

But careful: the try body could have `Nature{}` (unknown). `FromType(anyType)` is fine.

For the catch variable type: the error variable should be of type error (or any). Let me use `reflect.TypeOf((*error)(nil)).Elem()` = error interface.
`NtCache.NatureOf` with nil gives nil nature; better use `FromType(reflect.TypeOf((*error)(nil)).Elem())`. But `Nature` for interface may have `Nil`
flag etc. It should allow `errtype(err)` etc. Since `errtype` accepts any, fine. Also `err.Message`? The catch variable is an error; users may call
`string(err)`? There's no builtin for that. They can pass to errtype. Or use `err` in string concatenation? `err + ""`? Expr may not allow adding
error. Hmm. But the task only requires errtype classification. Also `catch err is "substr"` filters by message.

Let me set the catch variable type to `anyType` (interface{}) to be flexible. Then `errtype(err)` works. Actually error interface is also fine. Let's
use `anyType`.

But wait — how does the catch variable get its value? We'll store it in `vm.Variables[varIndex]`. The compiler needs to allocate a variable for the
catch name. In the compiler's TryNode, if Name != "", call `addVariable(node.Name)` and store the error there. The checker's varScopes must match the
compiler's variable index. The compiler tracks scopes with `beginScope(name, index)`. The checker tracks varScopes by name only (no index). That's
fine because compiler resolves by name at compile time.

So in compiler TryNode:
```go
func (c *compiler) TryNode(node *ast.TryNode) {
        // compile try
        tryStart := len(c.bytecode) + 1?
        ...
}
```

Let me design the compiler carefully. Let me define constants and layout.

```
OpTry <catchIP>            # arg = absolute ip of catch body (after OpJump)
<try body>
OpEndTry
OpJump <finallyOrEnd>      # success path
# catch body starts here (catchIP)
OpCatch
<catch body>
OpEndCatch
<finally body>
end:
```

Wait, but OpCatch as a separate opcode is a bit odd — it's just a marker executed on error path. We could instead have the VM, upon recovering, set a
flag and jump directly to catch body; then OpEndCatch marks the end. But we need OpCatch to mark the start so that on the success path's OpJump, the
catch start is known. Actually the compiler already knows catchIP. We don't need an OpCatch opcode; the VM just sets `frame.inCatch = true` when
recovering. We need OpEndCatch to mark end of catch body (pop frame). Let me add `OpEndCatch`. Hmm, but on the success path, the frame is popped by
OpEndTry. On error path, the frame remains until OpEndCatch. So we need OpEndCatch. We don't need OpCatch.

So layout:
```
OpTry <catchIP>
<try body>
OpEndTry          # success: pop frame
OpJump <finallyOrEnd>
catchIP:
<catch body>
OpEndCatch        # error path: pop frame
<finally body>
end:
```

But there's a problem: on the success path, `OpEndTry` pops the frame, then `OpJump` jumps to finally/end. On the error path, VM recovers and sets
`frame.inCatch=true`, jumps to catchIP (start of catch body). After catch body, OpEndCatch pops frame. Good.

But wait: what if the catch body calls `retry()`? OpRetry finds frame with inCatch=true. It must NOT pop it; it resets inCatch=false, increments
count, restores state, jumps to tryIP. Good.

Now, what about `OpEndTry` on the success path — it needs to pop the *top* frame. But what if there's a nested try inside the try body that's still
active? It would have been popped by its own OpEndTry on its success path. So the top frame is this try's. Good. But consider: the try body contains a
nested try that errors and is caught; nested catch ends with OpEndCatch popping nested frame. Good.

Now, how does OpRetry know `tryIP`? Store in frame.

Compiler:
```go
func (c *compiler) TryNode(node *ast.TryNode) {
        tryIP := len(c.bytecode) + 1 // position after OpTry? Actually OpTry will be at len(c.bytecode), and try body starts at len+1.
        c.emit(OpTry, placeholder)   // arg placeholder for catchIP
        // save tryIP for retry
        ...
        c.compile(node.Try)
        c.emit(OpEndTry)
        // success jump
        end := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
        // catch body
        catchIP := len(c.bytecode)
        c.arguments[tryIP-1] = catchIP  // patch OpTry arg
        if node.Name != "" {
                index := c.addVariable(node.Name)
                c.beginScope(node.Name, index)
                c.compile(node.Catch)
                c.endScope()
        } else {
                c.compile(node.Catch)
        }
        c.emit(OpEndCatch)
        // finally
        finallyStart := len(c.bytecode)
        if node.Finally != nil {
                c.compile(node.Finally)
        }
        c.patchJump(end) // success jump target
        _ = finallyStart
}
```

Wait, `patchJump(end)` sets `c.arguments[end-1] = len(c.bytecode) - end`. That's the offset from end to the current position (finally end / end).
Good.

But the catchIP stored in OpTry's arg is an absolute index. In VM handlePanic, `vm.ip = frame.catchIP`. Good.

There's a subtlety: `tryIP` should be the bytecode index where the try body starts, i.e., `len(c.bytecode)` after emitting OpTry (since emit appends).
Actually `c.emit(OpTry, placeholder)` appends OpTry at index `len(c.bytecode)-1`. So try body starts at `len(c.bytecode)` after that call. Let me set
`tryIP := len(c.bytecode)` after emitting OpTry.

Now, about `OpTry`'s arg and patching: we emit `OpTry` with `placeholder` as arg. Then later set `c.arguments[opTryIndex] = catchIP`. `opTryIndex` =
the index where OpTry was emitted = `len(c.bytecode)-1` at emit time. Let me store it.

Let me reconsider whether to store `catchIP` as absolute or relative. VM stores absolute; simpler. But the compiler's `placeholder` mechanism uses
relative offsets for jumps. For OpTry, we can set the absolute value directly since we know catchIP at compile time.

Now, the checker's tryNode needs to set the nature. Also, the parser must ensure exactly 2 args for `try(...)` and reject invalid.

If I make `is` an operator, this existing test would break. To avoid breaking existing behavior, I should NOT globally reserve `is`. Instead, the
parser can treat `is` as an identifier and only interpret it as the catch filter when parsing catch. I.e., after `catch <name>`, check if current
token is Identifier "is" (or maybe Operator if already emitted). Since `is` is lexed as Identifier normally, `catch err is "substr"` would parse:
after catch, current is Identifier "err" (name); next is Identifier "is"; we check `p.current.Is(Identifier, "is")` and consume; then expect String.
That works without reserving `is` as a keyword.

But what about `catch is "foo"`? Without a name, after `catch`, current is Identifier "is". We'd treat it as the name "is" (a variable named is), then
next is String, but we expect `{`. So it would fail. That's acceptable; spec requires a name before `is`.

So no lexer change needed for `is`. Just add `try`, `catch`, `finally` as operators? But `catch`/`finally` are only used in specific positions; if
someone uses `catch` as a variable name, it would break. But they're new keywords; reserving them is expected. However, the existing test uses `is` as
variable, so we must not reserve `is`. Let's reserve `try`, `catch`, `finally` (and maybe `retry` not—since it's a function call). Actually if `retry`
is a builtin function, and someone has an env variable `retry`, the parser's builtin check would override env? Let's check parser: in parseSecondary,
if identifier followed by `(`, it checks `predicates`, then `builtin.Index`, then creates CallNode. If `retry` is in builtin.Index, then `retry(...)`
always becomes BuiltinNode, even if env has retry. That could break existing users, but it's a new reserved builtin; acceptable. Actually expr
builtins can be overridden? In parseCall, `isOverridden = p.config.IsOverridden(token.Value)`. So if user uses `expr.Operator` or has env function,
builtin can be overridden. But not by plain env field function unless there's a mechanism. Actually config.IsOverridden checks Functions map (custom
functions). For env methods, there's separate handling in callNode. Let's not worry.

Now, should `retry` be lexed as identifier? Yes, so `retry()` parses as function call and builtin index makes it BuiltinNode. Good.

Should `try` be lexed as Operator or Identifier? If Identifier, then `try(...)` would parse as a normal CallNode and `try { }` would parse as... `try`
identifier followed by `{` — parseSecondary creates IdentifierNode, then parsePostfixExpression sees `{` (Bracket) which isn't a postfix, so node
returned; then parseExpression loop sees `{` not operator, returns; then parseSequence sees `{` and errors. So we need to handle `try` before
parseSecondary, whether as identifier or operator. If we lex `try` as Operator, parsePrimary can handle it. Either way. Let's reserve `try`, `catch`,
`finally` as operators in lexer (emit Operator). Then in parser parseExpression at precedence 0, if current is Operator "try", parse try.

But note: `catch` and `finally` as operators means they cannot be used as identifiers anywhere, including as env keys. That's a reasonable language
change for new keywords. Upstream did exactly this I believe.

### Compiler details for OpRetry, OpThrow

Let's add opcodes:
```
OpTry
OpEndTry
OpEndCatch
OpRetry
```
Also possibly `OpCatch` — no, not needed.

`OpThrow` currently pops an error and panics. We'll keep it; custom throw uses OpCall which panics with error, not OpThrow. OpThrow is used by
compiler for find/findLast/reduce errors (pushing error constants). It works.

`OpRetry`:
```go
case OpRetry:
        vm.retry(program)
```
`retry` method:
```go
func (vm *VM) retry(program *Program) {
        for i := len(vm.tries) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
                if vm.tries[i].inCatch {
                        frame := &vm.tries[i]
                        if frame.retries >= maxRetries {
                                panic(runtime.ErrRetryExhausted)
                        }
                        frame.retries++
                        frame.inCatch = false
                        // restore state
                        vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:frame.stackDepth]
                        vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:frame.scopesDepth]
                        ...currScope...
                        copy(vm.Variables, frame.variables) // restore
                        vm.ip = frame.tryIP
                        return
                }
        }
        panic("retry is not allowed outside a catch block")
}
```

Wait, but if we keep the frame on the stack during catch, then when retry sets inCatch=false and jumps to tryIP, the frame remains and is the active
try frame. Good.

But `OpEndCatch` pops the frame. What about the success path `OpEndTry` popping? Both pop.

However, there's a subtle issue with the panic handling: when a panic occurs inside the try body, we find the innermost frame with `!inCatch`, and if
it matches the substring filter, we set `inCatch=true` and jump to catch. But we must NOT pop the frame (we need it for retry). We do restore
stack/scopes/variables. Good. And we leave the frame on `vm.tries`. If the catch body finishes normally (OpEndCatch), we pop it. If the catch body
itself panics, the panic handler will skip frames with inCatch=true and look for an outer !inCatch frame. If none, re-panic. Good.

But wait — what about `finally`? After OpEndCatch pops the frame, finally runs. If finally throws, no frame for this try is active; an outer try
catches. Good.

What about the success path: OpEndTry pops frame, then OpJump to finally. Good.

Now, the `OpTry` frame saving variables. The frame needs a `variables []any` snapshot. On OpTry:
```go
case OpTry:
        frame := tryFrame{
                tryIP: vm.ip, // next instruction
                catchIP: arg,
                stackDepth: len(vm.Stack),
                scopesDepth: len(vm.Scopes),
                variables: append([]any(nil), vm.Variables...),
        }
        vm.tries = append(vm.tries, frame)
```
Wait, `vm.ip` at the time OpTry executes has already been incremented past OpTry, so `vm.ip` points to the try body start. Good.

But there's an issue: `vm.Scopes` restoration and `currScope`. When we truncate `vm.Scopes`, we must set `vm.currScope` accordingly. Also,
`scopePoolIdx` is not restored. That's fine (pool reuse; the truncated scopes remain allocated but unused). Actually, `allocScope` increments
`scopePoolIdx`; if we truncate scopes without decrementing scopePoolIdx, subsequent scopes in the same run use later pool slots — fine.

Now, what about the stack depth restore: if the try body pushed some values and then panicked, we truncate. Good.

What about `fnArgsBuf`? It's a local; not restored. Fine.

Now, the biggest question: does wrapping every opcode execution in a `recover()` closure cause performance issues? Upstream probably did something
similar. But we can optimize: only wrap when `len(vm.tries) > 0`. That's what I planned. There's a closure allocation per opcode when inside try, but
try blocks are typically small. Acceptable. Actually we could avoid closure allocation by making `execute` a method and using a helper that recovers.
But the closure is needed to capture program/env. Let me instead implement:

```go
func (vm *VM) executeOp(program *Program, env any, op Opcode, arg int, fnArgsBuf *[]any) {
        // big switch, return on handled
        ...
}
```

And in Run:
```go
for ... {
        ...
        if len(vm.tries) > 0 {
                func() {
                        defer func() {
                                if r := recover(); r != nil {
                                        vm.handlePanic(program, r)
                                }
                        }()
                        vm.executeOp(program, env, op, arg, &fnArgsBuf)
                }()
        } else {
                vm.executeOp(program, env, op, arg, &fnArgsBuf)
        }
}
```

The `handlePanic` may re-panic if no matching frame, which propagates to top-level recover. Good.

But note: `vm.handlePanic` itself could call `vm.push` and set `vm.ip`. After it returns, the loop continues at the new ip. Good.

Now, `OpEndTry` and `OpEndCatch` in executeOp:
```go
case OpEndTry:
        if len(vm.tries) == 0 { panic("OpEndTry without OpTry") }
        vm.tries = vm.tries[:len(vm.tries)-1]
case OpEndCatch:
        if len(vm.tries) == 0 || !vm.tries[len(vm.tries)-1].inCatch {
                panic("OpEndCatch without catch")
        }
        vm.tries = vm.tries[:len(vm.tries)-1]
```

Wait, on the success path, we call OpEndTry which pops the top frame. But is the top frame necessarily this try's? Yes, if nested tries are properly
balanced. But what if the try body contains a nested try that's still in catch? No, nested catch must have ended before the outer try body ends
(lexical). So top is this frame. Good.

But there's a subtlety: on the success path, after OpEndTry, we jump to finally. On the error path, after catch body, OpEndCatch pops. Good.

Now, `handlePanic`:
```go
func (vm *VM) handlePanic(program *Program, r any) {
        err := runtime.AsError(r)  // convert
        for i := len(vm.tries) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
                frame := &vm.tries[i]
                if frame.inCatch {
                        continue
                }
                if frame.substr != "" && !strings.Contains(err.Error(), frame.substr) {
                        continue
                }
                // match: restore state to frame
                vm.tries = vm.tries[:i+1] // keep frames up to and including this one; discard inner frames
                vm.tries[i].inCatch = true
                vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:frame.stackDepth]
                vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:frame.scopesDepth]
                if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 { vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1] } else { vm.currScope = nil }
                copy(vm.Variables, frame.variables)
                if frame.name != "" {
                        vm.Variables[frame.varIndex] = err
                } else {
                        vm.push(err)
                }
                vm.ip = frame.catchIP
                return
        }
        panic(r)
}
```

Wait — `frame` is a pointer into `vm.tries[i]`, but if we do `vm.tries = vm.tries[:i+1]`, that's fine as long as i+1 <= len. But if we set
`vm.tries[i].inCatch = true` after truncating, still fine. But note: when we restore `vm.Scopes` and `vm.Stack`, and `vm.ip`, we need `frame`'s fields
still accessible; pointer remains valid since we keep the slice up to i+1. Good.

But there's a subtle bug: if inner frames existed (i < len-1) and we truncate to i+1, the inner frames are discarded. Their stack/scopes state was
already unwound as part of the panic (the panic unwound through them without catching). Since we restore to frame's saved depths, that's correct.
Good.

Now, for `retry`, similar restore but keep frame and set inCatch=false.

What about `catch` variable storage: `frame.varIndex`. The compiler assigns the variable index. We store the error there. But note: variables snapshot
was taken at OpTry; when we restore on error, then write the error into `vm.Variables[varIndex]`, it persists. The catch body reads it via OpLoadVar.
Good. When retry happens, `OpRetry` restores variables to snapshot (which does NOT include the error) — good, so the catch variable is cleared. But
wait, the frame's `variables` snapshot was taken at OpTry, before any let declarations in try body. So restoring clears those. Good.

Hmm, but there's a problem: the `variables` snapshot is taken at OpTry, but the catch variable index might be > len(snapshot) if the variable is
allocated later? No — variables are allocated at compile time; the VM's `vm.Variables` is sized to `program.variables` before the loop. So
`vm.Variables[varIndex]` exists. The snapshot copies the whole slice including zero values. Good.

Now, let's think about `finally` and the `try(...)` function form. Function form `try(expr, fallback)` has no catch name; on error, push error? No —
function form should return fallback result, not expose the error. The catch body for function form is just `fallback` expression. It doesn't bind the
error. So frame.name="" and on error we `vm.push(err)` — wait, but the catch body for function form is the fallback expression, which would then have
the error on the stack as an extra value! We don't want that.

Hmm. Let me reconsider the stack protocol. For a named catch, on error we store error in variable (no push). For an unnamed catch, how does the catch
body access the error? In `try { } catch { }` without a name, the handler doesn't need the error. So on error we should NOT push the error; the catch
body just evaluates. For function form `try(expr, fallback)`, same — fallback doesn't need the error. So: if `name == ""`, don't push error at all; if
`name != ""`, store in variable.

But wait, does the task require unnamed catch to be able to access the error? No — `catch { }` just handles. To access, use `catch <name>`. So no push
needed. Good. That simplifies: on error, if name != "", store; else nothing. The stack is restored to saved depth (which is the state after OpTry —
i.e., before try body). Then catch body compiles on top. Good.

Now, for function form, the parser builds TryNode with Try=expr1, Catch=expr2, Name="", Finally=nil. Compiler emits try/catch with catch body = expr2.
On success, expr1 result remains on stack. On error, stack restored, catch body (fallback) evaluates and leaves result. Both paths then... wait, the
layout: on success, after try body, OpEndTry, OpJump to end (after catch). The try body's result stays on stack. On error, catch body evaluates and
leaves result on stack. Then both converge at end. Good.

But hold on: after `OpEndTry` on success, we do `OpJump <end>`. The try result remains on the stack. The OpJump skips the catch body. Good. The end
point is after OpEndCatch. So the stack has one value. Good.

Now, the compiler's `TryNode` for the function form: catch body = fallback expression. No name. Good.

Now, the success path jump: in the layout, after `OpEndTry`, `OpJump <finallyOrEnd>`. The catch body is at catchIP. If there's a finally, the success
jump targets finally start; catch body falls through OpEndCatch into finally. Both paths execute finally. Good.

But wait: on success, after OpEndTry pops frame, OpJump to finally. On error, catch body → OpEndCatch pops frame → falls into finally. Both execute
finally. But the stack after success has the try result; after error has the catch result. The finally body will push its own result on top and...
it's the last expression, so the final result is the finally result? Hmm! We need finally to NOT affect the result unless it throws. The task:
"`finally { cleanup }` - optional clause that always executes after try/catch; if the finally body throws, that error propagates (overriding any prior
result)." This implies the finally's return value is discarded; the try/catch result is preserved. So after finally executes, we need to discard the
finally result and keep the try/catch result underneath.

How to arrange the stack so that finally's result is discarded but the prior result stays? We can save the prior result before finally, execute
finally, pop finally result, then push the saved result back.

Layout with finally:
```
OpTry <catchIP>
<try body>                 # leaves result
OpEndTry
OpJump <finallyOrEnd>
catchIP:
<catch body>               # leaves result
OpEndCatch
finallyOrEnd:
# if finally exists:
...
```

Hmm, both paths reach finally with a result on the stack. We need to preserve it. Approach: before executing finally, save the result by swapping to a
variable or by stack manipulation. Since we know there's exactly one value on the stack at that point (the result), we can do:

```
# at finally start:
OpStore <tmpVar>   # pop result into a temp variable
<finally body>
OpPop              # discard finally result
OpLoadVar <tmpVar> # push back saved result
```

But this requires allocating a temp variable at compile time, and the finally body could throw; if it throws, the temp var holds the prior result but
it's discarded — that's fine, the new error propagates. If finally succeeds, we load the saved result. But what if the try/catch result is nil and
`OpStore` stores nil? Fine.

Alternatively, we can structure using stack: at finally start, we can leave the result on the stack, compile finally, then after finally, pop the
finally result, then... but the finally result is on top, and the try result is underneath. So:
```
<finally body>     # pushes result on top
OpPop              # discard finally result
```
Then the try/catch result remains underneath! Wait, that's simpler. Let's verify: At finally start, stack = [result]. We execute finally body which
pushes its result: stack = [result, finally_result]. Then `OpPop` pops finally_result, leaving [result].

But is the finally body guaranteed to leave exactly one value on the stack? Yes, any expression evaluates to one value (SequenceNode pops
intermediate). But what if the finally body itself is empty? `finally { }` — an empty block. Parser's `parseSequenceExpression` would parse... hmm, an
empty block would be a parse error (parseExpression on `}` errors). So finally body must be a valid expression. Fine.

So the simple approach: on success path, jump to finally start with result on stack; compile finally body; then `OpPop` to discard its result. On
error path, catch body leaves result, OpEndCatch, then falls into finally body, then OpPop. Both paths converge after OpPop.

But wait, there's a catch: if the try body panics, we restore the stack to saved depth, so at catch start the stack is empty (no result). Catch body
leaves result. Then finally runs on top. Good.

So compiler TryNode with finally:
```
OpTry <catchIP>
<try>
OpEndTry
OpJump <afterCatch>     # success: jump over catch to finally start
catchIP:
<catch>
OpEndCatch
afterCatch:
# finally start
if finally:
  <finally>
  OpPop
```
Wait, the success path jumps to afterCatch (finally start), and catch path falls through afterCatch too. Both then run finally and OpPop.

But where is `afterCatch`? It's right after OpEndCatch. The success OpJump targets it. Let me define:
- `success := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)` after OpEndTry.
- catch body, then `c.emit(OpEndCatch)`.
- `c.patchJump(success)` → targets current position (after OpEndCatch).
- if finally: compile finally, then `OpPop`.

But wait: on success, after OpEndTry, we jump to after OpEndCatch. On error, catch body runs, OpEndCatch, then falls to afterOpEndCatch. Both
converge. Good.

What if there's no finally? Then after OpEndCatch, we're at end. Success jump targets there. No OpPop. Good.

Now, an important subtlety: the `OpEndTry` on the success path is placed right after the try body. But the try body might contain control flow like
`if` that leaves the result on the stack — fine. It's linear.

Now, what about `OpEndCatch` placement and the catch body's stack? The catch body may itself contain a try/catch; those are balanced within. Fine.

Now, `OpEndCatch` is only executed on the error path. On success path we jump over it. Good.

Let me also consider: what if the catch body has `retry()` at the end? `OpRetry` sets vm.ip to tryIP and returns from executeOp; it does NOT execute
OpEndCatch. The frame remains (inCatch=false). Good.

What if the catch body calls `retry()` but the try body succeeds on retry? Then success path: try body result on stack, OpEndTry pops frame, OpJump to
end. Good.

What if retry count exceeded? OpRetry panics with ErrRetryExhausted. This panic occurs inside catch (frame.inCatch=true). The panic handler skips
inCatch frames and looks for outer. If no outer, re-panic → top-level. If outer exists, outer catches it and classifies errtype as "retry". Good.

Now, what about `try { } catch err { retry() }` where the try body keeps failing and after 3 retries exhausts — the exhaustion error should propagate
(or be caught by outer try). The frame's retries count increments on each retry. On the 4th `retry()` call (after initial + 3 retries), `frame.retries
>= 3` → panic. So max 3 retries. Good.

Now, let's think about `OpTry` and `OpEndTry` with nested try where the inner try is inside the outer try body. Frame stack: outer OpTry pushes frame,
inner OpTry pushes frame. If inner catches, its frame stays (inCatch) through catch, then OpEndCatch pops. If inner try body succeeds, OpEndTry pops.
If inner try body panics and no matching catch, the panic handler skips inner (if substring mismatch, it `continue`s), then finds outer frame,
restores outer state, truncates frames to outer (discarding inner). Good.

Now, an edge: `try { try { throw("x") } catch e is "nomatch" { } } catch { ... }`. Inner catch doesn't match → panic propagates to outer. The outer
frame restores stack to outer saved depth (discarding inner frame and inner catch etc.), catches. Good.

Now, what about `finally` and `retry` interplay: If catch calls retry, finally does not run on each retry attempt? The task says finally "always
executes after try/catch". On retry, we're re-entering the try body, not completing the try/catch, so finally should not run until the try/catch fully
completes (success or final error propagation). Our design: retry jumps back to tryIP, skipping finally. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: On error path, we restore stack to `frame.stackDepth` which was captured at OpTry (before try body). But what if the try
expression itself is part of a larger expression that had values on the stack before OpTry? E.g., `1 + try(2/0, 3)`. The compiler compiles `1` (push
1), then compiles TryNode. At OpTry, stackDepth = 1 (the `1`). On error, restore to depth 1, then catch body (fallback 3) pushes 3 → stack [1,3], then
OpAdd. Good. On success, try body 2/0 leaves result, OpEndTry, jump over catch, stack [1,result]. Good.

Now, let's consider the `try` function form exactly. Parser: `try(expr1, expr2)`. We need to ensure exactly 2 args. Let me implement `parseTry`:
```go
func (p *Parser) parseTry() Node {
        token := p.current
        p.next()
        if p.current.Is(Bracket, "(") {
                p.next()
                tryExpr := p.parseExpression(0)
                p.expect(Operator, ",")
                catchExpr := p.parseExpression(0)
                if !p.current.Is(Bracket, ")") {
                        p.error("try() requires exactly two arguments")
                        // consume rest?
                }
                p.expect(Bracket, ")")
                return p.createNode(&TryNode{Try: tryExpr, Catch: catchExpr}, token.Location)
        }
        // block form
        p.expect(Bracket, "{")
        tryBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
        p.expect(Bracket, "}")
        p.expect(Operator, "catch")
        ...
}
```

But there's an issue: after `try` is consumed, `p.next()` moves to next token. For `try(`, current is `(`. For `try {`, current is `{`.

For catch parsing:
```go
var name string
if p.current.Is(Identifier) && !p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
        name = p.current.Value
        p.next()
}
// optional is "substr"
var substr string
var hasSubstr bool
if p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
        if name == "" { p.error("catch is requires a name") }
        p.next()
        strToken := p.current
        p.expect(String)
        substr = strToken.Value
        hasSubstr = true
}
p.expect(Bracket, "{")
catchBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
p.expect(Bracket, "}")
// optional finally
var finallyBody Node
if p.current.Is(Operator, "finally") {
        p.next()
        p.expect(Bracket, "{")
        finallyBody = p.parseSequenceExpression()
        p.expect(Bracket, "}")
}
return p.createNode(&TryNode{Try: tryBody, Catch: catchBody, Finally: finallyBody, Name: name, Substr: substr, HasSubstr: hasSubstr}, token.Location)
```

Wait — `is` is lexed as Identifier. But what if a user has `catch err { ... }` and `is` never appears. Fine. What if the catch body expression starts
with an identifier that is `is`? Not relevant since we parse `{` after.

One issue: `p.current.Is(Identifier)` includes keywords true/false/nil? No, those are identifiers but handled in parseSecondary. If someone writes
`catch nil { }`, we'd treat `nil` as the name. That's weird but acceptable; or we could restrict to non-keywords. Let's allow any identifier as name
(including true/false/nil), but those would shadow. Actually better to require a valid identifier that's not a literal. Let me check
`utils.IsValidIdentifier`. The lexer already emits Identifier for all words including true/false/nil. Let me just accept any Identifier as name except
`is`. Simple.

But there's an ambiguity: `catch { ... }` — current after catch is `{`, so we skip name. Good. `catch err is "x" { ... }` — current is `err`
identifier, name=err, next is `is`, parse substr. Good. `catch err { ... }` — name=err, next is `{`, no substr. Good.

Now, what about `catch is { ... }` where the catch variable is named `is`? It would be parsed as name="is", then next is `{`, no substr. That actually
works! But then `catch is "foo" { }` would parse name="is", then next is String, not `{`, so we'd try `p.expect(Bracket, "{")` and error. That's a
weird edge; spec says `catch <name> is "substring"`, so if name is `is`, ambiguity. Acceptable. Since `is` isn't reserved, `catch is { }` could mean
variable named is. Fine.

Now, how does parseExpression know to call parseTry? At precedence 0:
```go
if precedence == 0 && p.current.Is(Operator, "try") {
        return p.parseTry()
}
```
Place before parsePrimary, after let/if handling.

Now, `try` as operator in lexer. Also `catch`, `finally` as operators. Let's modify lexer state.go identifier switch:
```go
case "try", "catch", "finally":
        l.emit(Operator)
```

Now `retry` — it's an identifier, and builtin.Index will include "retry", so parser creates BuiltinNode.

### Builtin registration

Add to builtin.Builtins:
```go
{
        Name: "throw",
        Func: func(args ...any) (any, error) {
                if len(args) != 1 {
                        return nil, fmt.Errorf("invalid number of arguments (expected 1, got %d)", len(args))
                }
                return nil, &runtime.RuntimeError{Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", args[0])}
        },
        Types: types(new(func(any) any)),
        Validate: func(args []reflect.Type) (reflect.Type, error) {
                if len(args) != 1 {
                        return anyType, fmt.Errorf("invalid number of arguments (expected 1, got %d)", len(args))
                }
                return anyType, nil
        },
},
{
        Name: "retry",
        Validate: func(args []reflect.Type) (reflect.Type, error) {
                if len(args) != 0 {
                        return anyType, fmt.Errorf("invalid number of arguments (expected 0, got %d)", len(args))
                }
                return anyType, nil
        },
        Types: types(new(func() any)),
},
{
        Name: "errtype",
        Fast: ErrType,
        Types: types(new(func(any) string)),
        Validate: func(args []reflect.Type) (reflect.Type, error) {
                if len(args) != 1 {
                        return stringType, fmt.Errorf("invalid number of arguments (expected 1, got %d)", len(args))
                }
                return stringType, nil
        },
},
```

But `retry` has no Func/Fast/Safe — the compiler will special-case it before reaching the generic builtin compilation. In the compiler's BuiltinNode,
at the top of the switch (after special builtins), add:
```go
case "retry":
        if len(node.Arguments) != 0 {
                panic(...) // should never happen due to checker
        }
        c.emit(OpRetry)
        return
```

Actually, we should handle `retry` before the `if id, ok := builtin.Index[node.Name]` generic path, because generic path would emit nothing if f.Func
is nil. Let me add `case "retry":` in the BuiltinNode switch.

For `throw`, the generic path works (Func != nil). For `errtype`, generic path works (Fast != nil).

But wait: `throw` returning `(nil, error)` — when called via OpCall1, the VM does:
```go
out, err := program.functions[arg](args...)
if err != nil { panic(err) }
vm.push(out)
```
So it panics with the error. Good. The error type is `*runtime.RuntimeError`.

  1148				}
  1149			}
  1150

  1152			// We check original type (like *time.Time), not dereferenced type,

REASONING_CONTENT
If we define `Validate`, checkFunction uses Validate and returns `FromType(t)`. It doesn't call `Types` at all. So for `throw`, `errtype`, `retry`, if
we provide Validate, the Types won't be used. Good. But for `retry`, since it has no Func and Validate returns anyType, checker works. The compiler
special-cases. Good.

But wait — for builtins with Validate, `checkFunction` calls `v.visit(arg)` for each argument. For `retry()` with 0 args, no visits. Good.

Now, there's also the issue that `builtin.Index` maps name → index. The parser uses `builtin.Index[token.Value]` to decide BuiltinNode. Good.

Now, `errtype` Fast: `ErrType(arg any) any` returns string. The compiler's generic builtin path: `if f.Fast != nil { c.emit(OpCallBuiltin1, id) }`. It
compiles the single argument (after deref). At runtime, `OpCallBuiltin1: vm.push(builtin.Builtins[arg].Fast(vm.pop()))`. Good.

Now, `throw`: the generic builtin path: f.Func != nil, calls `emitFunction(f, len(args))` → OpCall1. At runtime `program.functions[idx](arg)` returns
`(nil, err)`, VM panics with err. Good. But wait — the compiler's generic path also handles deref: for each arg, if arg type is Ptr or unknown, emit
OpDeref. For `throw(x)` with x unknown, it emits OpDeref. Fine.

Now, there's one problem: `throw` returns `(any, error)` and the type is `func(any) any` per Types. But with Validate, Types isn't used. The checker
returns anyType. Compiler's generic path checks `arg.Type()` etc. Fine.

### RuntimeError and AsError

Let me define in `vm/runtime` a new file:
```go
package runtime

import (
        "errors"
        "fmt"
)

// RuntimeError represents an error thrown by the throw() builtin.
type RuntimeError struct {
        Message string
}

func (e *RuntimeError) Error() string { return e.Message }

var ErrRetryExhausted = errors.New("retry limit exceeded")

// AsError converts a recovered panic value into an error.
func AsError(r any) error {
        if e, ok := r.(error); ok {
                return e
        }
        if s, ok := r.(string); ok {
                return errors.New(s)
        }
        return errors.New(fmt.Sprintf("%v", r))
}
```

Let me write `ErrType`:
```go
func ErrType(arg any) any {
        if arg == nil {
                return "none"
        }
        var message string
        switch v := arg.(type) {
        case error:
                message = v.Error()
        default:
                message = fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)
        }

        if _, ok := arg.(*runtime.RuntimeError); ok {
                return "custom"
        }

        msg := strings.ToLower(message)
        switch {
        case strings.Contains(msg, "out of range"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "bounds out of range"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "out-of-range"):
                return "index"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "conversion"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot convert"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation: int("),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation: int64("),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation: float("),
                strings.Contains(msg, "strconv."),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid syntax"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot parse"):
                return "conversion"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "nil pointer"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid memory address"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "is nil"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot call nil"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "nil map"):
                return "nil"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "retry"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "retry limit"):
                return "retry"
        case strings.Contains(msg, "interface conversion"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "type assertion"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot use"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "mismatched type"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "is not callable"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot call non-function"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "not defined on"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid argument for"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot fetch"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot get"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "cannot slice"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "no field"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "no method"),
                strings.Contains(msg, "invalid operation:"):
                return "type"
        default:
                return "custom"
        }
}
```

Need `strings` import in lib.go.

package optimizer

import (
	"math"

	. "github.com/expr-lang/expr/ast"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/file"
)

type fold struct {
	applied bool
	err     *file.Error
}

func (fold *fold) Visit(node *Node) {
	patch := func(newNode Node) {
		fold.applied = true
		patchWithType(node, newNode)
	}
	patchCopy := func(newNode Node) {
		fold.applied = true
		patchCopyType(node, newNode)
	}

	switch n := (*node).(type) {
	case *UnaryNode:
		switch n.Operator {
		case "-":
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*IntegerNode); ok {
				patch(&IntegerNode{Value: -i.Value})
			}
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*FloatNode); ok {
				patch(&FloatNode{Value: -i.Value})
			}
		case "+":
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*IntegerNode); ok {
				patch(&IntegerNode{Value: i.Value})
			}
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*FloatNode); ok {
				patch(&FloatNode{Value: i.Value})
			}
		case "!", "not":
			if a := toBool(n.Node); a != nil {
				patch(&BoolNode{Value: !a.Value})
			}
		}

	case *BinaryNode:
		switch n.Operator {

				b := toInteger(n.Right)

				}
			}
			{
				a := toInteger(n.Left)
				b := toFloat(n.Right)

package optimizer

import (
	"fmt"
	"reflect"

	. "github.com/expr-lang/expr/ast"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/conf"
)

func Optimize(node *Node, config *conf.Config) error {
	Walk(node, &inArray{})
	for limit := 1000; limit >= 0; limit-- {
		fold := &fold{}
		Walk(node, fold)
		if fold.err != nil {
			return fold.err
		}
		if !fold.applied {
			break
		}
	}
	if config != nil && len(config.ConstFns) > 0 {
		for limit := 100; limit >= 0; limit-- {
			constExpr := &constExpr{
				fns: config.ConstFns,
			}
			Walk(node, constExpr)
			if constExpr.err != nil {
				return constExpr.err
			}
			if !constExpr.applied {
				break
			}
		}
	}
	Walk(node, &inRange{})
	Walk(node, &filterMap{})
	Walk(node, &filterLen{})
	Walk(node, &filterLast{})
	Walk(node, &filterFirst{})
	Walk(node, &predicateCombination{})
	Walk(node, &sumRange{})
	Walk(node, &sumArray{})
	Walk(node, &sumMap{})
	Walk(node, &countAny{})
	Walk(node, &countThreshold{})
	return nil
}

var (
	boolType    = reflect.TypeOf(true)
	integerType = reflect.TypeOf(0)
	floatType   = reflect.TypeOf(float64(0))
	stringType  = reflect.TypeOf("")
)

func patchWithType(node *Node, newNode Node) {
	switch n := newNode.(type) {
	case *BoolNode:
		newNode.SetType(boolType)
	case *IntegerNode:
		newNode.SetType(integerType)
	case *FloatNode:
		newNode.SetType(floatType)
	case *StringNode:
		newNode.SetType(stringType)
	case *ConstantNode:
		newNode.SetType(reflect.TypeOf(n.Value))
	case *BinaryNode:
		newNode.SetType(n.Type())
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("unknown type %T", newNode))
	}
	Patch(node, newNode)
}

func patchCopyType(node *Node, newNode Node) {
	t := (*node).Type()
	newNode.SetType(t)
	Patch(node, newNode)
}

package optimizer

import (
	"reflect"

	. "github.com/expr-lang/expr/ast"
)

type inArray struct{}

func (*inArray) Visit(node *Node) {
	switch n := (*node).(type) {
	case *BinaryNode:
		if n.Operator == "in" {
			if array, ok := n.Right.(*ArrayNode); ok {
				if len(array.Nodes) > 0 {
					t := n.Left.Type()
					if t == nil || t.Kind() != reflect.Int {
						// This optimization can be only performed if left side is int type,
						// as runtime.in func uses reflect.Map.MapIndex and keys of map must,
						// be same as checked value type.
						goto string

							goto string

						patchCopyType(node, &BinaryNode{
							Operator: n.Operator,
							Left:     n.Left,
							Right:    m,
						})
					}

					for _, a := range array.Nodes {
						if _, ok := a.(*StringNode); !ok {
							return
						}
					}

cd /app && cat >> ast/node.go <<'EOF'

// TryNode represents a try/catch expression.
// It can be used in two forms:
//
//	try(expr, fallback)
//
// or:
//
//	try { expr } catch [name] [is "substring"] { handler } [finally { cleanup }]
type TryNode struct {
	base
	Try       Node   // Expression to evaluate.
	Catch     Node   // Handler evaluated on error.
	Finally   Node   // Cleanup evaluated after both paths (may be nil).
	Name      string // Optional catch variable name.
	Substr    string // Optional substring to filter errors (ignored if empty).
	HasSubstr bool   // Whether Substr is set.
}
EOF

	case *TryNode:
		Walk(&n.Try, v)
		if n.Catch != nil {
			Walk(&n.Catch, v)
		}
		if n.Finally != nil {
			Walk(&n.Finally, v)
		}
	default:""")
open(p,'w').write(s)
PY
cat >> ast/print.go <<'EOF'

func (n *TryNode) String() string {
	var b strings.Builder
	if n.Catch != nil && n.Name == "" && n.Substr == "" && n.Finally == nil {
		// Function form: try(expr, fallback)
		b.WriteString("try(")
		b.WriteString(n.Try.String())
		b.WriteString(", ")
		b.WriteString(n.Catch.String())
		b.WriteString(")")
		return b.String()
	}
	b.WriteString("try { ")
	b.WriteString(n.Try.String())
	b.WriteString(" } catch")
	if n.Name != "" {
		b.WriteString(" ")
		b.WriteString(n.Name)
		if n.HasSubstr {
			b.WriteString(" is ")
			b.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("%q", n.Substr))
		}
	}
	b.WriteString(" { ")
	if n.Catch != nil {
		b.WriteString(n.Catch.String())
	}
	b.WriteString(" }")
	if n.Finally != nil {
		b.WriteString(" finally { ")
		b.WriteString(n.Finally.String())
		b.WriteString(" }")
	}
	return b.String()
}
EOF

assert old in s
s=s.replace(old,new)
# add parseTry method after parseConditionalIf
old='''func (p *Parser) parseConditional(node Node) Node {'''
new='''func (p *Parser) parseTry() Node {
	token := p.current
	p.next()
	if p.err != nil {
		return nil
	}

	// Function form: try(expr, fallback)
	if p.current.Is(Bracket, "(") {
		p.next()
		tryExpr := p.parseExpression(0)
		p.expect(Operator, ",")
		catchExpr := p.parseExpression(0)
		if !p.current.Is(Bracket, ")") {
			p.error("try() requires exactly two arguments")
		}
		p.expect(Bracket, ")")
		if tryExpr == nil || catchExpr == nil {
			return nil
		}
		return p.createNode(&TryNode{
			Try:   tryExpr,
			Catch: catchExpr,
		}, token.Location)
	}

	// Block form: try { ... } catch [name] [is "substr"] { ... } [finally { ... }]
	p.expect(Bracket, "{")
	tryBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
	p.expect(Bracket, "}")
	p.expect(Operator, "catch")

	var name string
	if p.current.Is(Identifier) && p.current.Value != "is" {
		name = p.current.Value
		p.next()
	}

	var substr string
	hasSubstr := false
	if p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
		if name == "" {
			p.error("catch clause with substring filter requires an error name")
		}
		p.next()
		strToken := p.current
		if !strToken.Is(String) {
			p.error("expected string after is")
		}
		substr = strToken.Value
		hasSubstr = true
		p.next()
	}

	p.expect(Bracket, "{")
	catchBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
	p.expect(Bracket, "}")

	var finallyBody Node
	if p.current.Is(Operator, "finally") {
		p.next()
		p.expect(Bracket, "{")
		finallyBody = p.parseSequenceExpression()
		p.expect(Bracket, "}")
	}

	if tryBody == nil || catchBody == nil {
		return nil
	}
	return p.createNode(&TryNode{
		Try:       tryBody,
		Catch:     catchBody,
		Finally:   finallyBody,
		Name:      name,
		Substr:    substr,
		HasSubstr: hasSubstr,
	}, token.Location)
}

		nt = v.pairNode(n)
	case *ast.TryNode:
		nt = v.tryNode(n)

assert old in s

# add tryNode method before pairNode method (find pairNode)
marker='''func (v *Checker) pairNode(node *ast.PairNode) Nature {'''
assert marker in s
method='''func (v *Checker) tryNode(node *ast.TryNode) Nature {
	tryNT := v.visit(node.Try)

	var catchNT Nature
	if node.Catch != nil {
		if node.Name != "" {
			v.varScopes = append(v.varScopes, varScope{
				name:   node.Name,
				nature: v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType),
			})
		}
		catchNT = v.visit(node.Catch)
		if node.Name != "" {
			v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]
		}
	}

	if node.Finally != nil {
		_ = v.visit(node.Finally)
	}

	// A try/catch expression's type is the common type of its two branches.
	if catchNT.Type == nil || catchNT.Type == anyType {
		return tryNT
	}
	if tryNT.Type == nil || tryNT.Type == anyType {
		return catchNT
	}
	if tryNT.Type == catchNT.Type {
		return tryNT
	}
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}

'''
s=s.replace(marker, method+marker, 1)
open(p,'w').write(s)
print("ok")

	"fmt"
)

// RuntimeError is the error type produced by the throw() builtin.
// It is classified as "custom" by the errtype() builtin.
type RuntimeError struct {
	Message string
}

func (e *RuntimeError) Error() string {
	return e.Message
}

// ErrRetryExhausted is returned by retry() after the retry limit is reached.
var ErrRetryExhausted = errors.New("retry limit exceeded")

// AsError converts a recovered panic value into an error value.
func AsError(r any) error {
	if e, ok := r.(error); ok {
		return e
	}
	if s, ok := r.(string); ok {
		return errors.New(s)
	}
	return errors.New(fmt.Sprintf("%v", r))
}
EOF

```go
type tryFrame struct {

        catchIP    int

        scopesDepth int
```

```go
                tryIP:      vm.ip,

```

re-panicking needs r. Good.

Now, how to convert the recovered panic to error and store. `handlePanic`:
```go
func (vm *VM) handlePanic(r any) {
        err := runtime.AsError(r)
        msg := err.Error()
        for i := len(vm.tries) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
                frame := &vm.tries[i]
                if frame.inCatch {
                        continue
                }
                if frame.substr != "" && !strings.Contains(msg, frame.substr) {
                        continue
                }
                // Discard inner frames.
                vm.tries = vm.tries[:i+1]
                frame = &vm.tries[i]
                frame.inCatch = true
                vm.restoreTryState(frame)
                if frame.name != "" {
                        vm.Variables[frame.varIndex] = err
                }
                vm.ip = frame.catchIP
                return
        }
        panic(r)
}
```

`restoreTryState`:
```go
func (vm *VM) restoreTryState(frame *tryFrame) {
        vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:frame.stackDepth]
        vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:frame.scopesDepth]
        if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
                vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
        } else {
                vm.currScope = nil
        }
        copy(vm.Variables, frame.variables)
}
```

Wait, `copy(vm.Variables, frame.variables)` — if frame.variables is shorter than vm.Variables (it won't be; it's a copy of full slice), but if
vm.Variables grew after snapshot (it doesn't grow during run; it's sized at start), fine. But if `frame.variables` is a snapshot of `vm.Variables`
which might be length < program.variables if... no, it's sized before loop. Good.

`retry`:
```go
func (vm *VM) retry() {
        for i := len(vm.tries) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
                if vm.tries[i].inCatch {
                        frame := &vm.tries[i]
                        if frame.retries >= maxRetries {
                                panic(runtime.ErrRetryExhausted)
                        }
                        frame.retries++
                        frame.inCatch = false
                        vm.restoreTryState(frame)
                        vm.ip = frame.tryIP
                        return
                }
        }
        panic("retry is not allowed outside a catch block")
}
```

const maxRetries = 3.

Now, a subtlety: when `OpRetry` executes, it's inside `executeOp`, and `vm.ip` has already been incremented past OpRetry. We set vm.ip = frame.tryIP.
After executeOp returns, the loop continues at tryIP. Good.

Now, there's an issue with the per-opcode recover wrapper and `OpRetry` panic: `OpRetry` may panic (retry exhaustion or outside catch). If inside a
try, this panic gets caught by the wrapper and `handlePanic` runs. Since retry-exhaustion panic occurs inside catch (frame.inCatch=true), handlePanic
skips it and checks outer frames. Good. If outside catch with no active try, `len(vm.tries)>0` might be false, so no wrapper; panic goes to top-level.
Good.

Now, an important edge: when `handlePanic` restores state, it should also clear any stale `fnArgsBuf`? Not necessary.

Another edge: `vm.tries` reset at start of Run. Add `vm.tries = vm.tries[:0]`.

Now, let me also consider the `debug` mode: `Debug()` creates VM with debug true. Our changes work.

Now, one concern: The closure `func() { defer ...; vm.executeOp(...) }()` is allocated each opcode inside try. That's fine functionally.

    40		Stack        []any

    53		scopePoolIdx int     // Current index into scopePool for allocation

    56		tries        []tryFrame
