Now let me review the final list of changes and consider whether to add a CHANGELOG or anything. Not necessary.

Let me also reconsider the **`errtype` for "type" category including "cannot fetch" from non-nil wrong types**. For `try { 1.Foo }` where 1 is int —
runtime.Fetch(1, "Foo") → "cannot fetch Foo from int" → classified "type". Good. For nil pointer, we now generate "from nil" → "nil". Good.

Now, one more subtle thing: **`runtime.Fetch` nil check order**: We added the nil check after the invalid check. For `from` = nil untyped,
`reflect.ValueOf(nil)` → invalid → first panic "cannot fetch ... from <nil>" (no nil pointer). Classifier handles "from <nil>" → nil. For `from` = nil
*S, reflect.ValueOf gives Ptr Kind with IsNil → our new panic "cannot fetch ... from nil *main.S". Classifier handles "from nil" → nil. Good.

Let me verify that changing runtime.Fetch error messages for nil pointers didn't break fuzz tests (they passed). Good.

Now, let me think about **whether `try` should be listed as an operator in the parser's operator table** — no, it's handled specially. Good.

Now, let me consider **the `OpTry` constant being a `*TryInfo` pointer stored in program.Constants**. The `addConstant` deduplicates indexable types.
A `*TryInfo` pointer is comparable, so two different try blocks with different pointers won't dedup (different pointer values). But could two
identical `*TryInfo` pointers dedup if somehow reused? No, each `&TryInfo{}` is a new pointer. Fine.

But there's a potential issue: `addConstant` uses `reflect.TypeOf(constant).Kind()` — for `*TryInfo`, Kind is Ptr, which is indexable, so it tries to
use the pointer as a map key. That's fine. But the `hash` is the pointer; two pointers are different. Good.

Now, let me also check **`c.arguments[opTry-1] = c.addConstant(...)`** — `addConstant` appends to `c.constants` and returns index. Setting it after
all compilation is fine. But note: `addConstant` for a pointer is "indexable" so it also stores in `constantsIndex`. If the same pointer is added
again it would return the same index. Not an issue.

Now, let me verify **the disassembler handles OpTry's argument correctly**. In program.go, for OpTry I used `code("OpTry")` which just prints the
opcode, not the argument. That's fine; could show the constant but not necessary.

Now, let me think about **`vm/tries` and `memory budget` with infinite retries**: retry max 3 prevents infinite loops. But what if the try body itself
has a loop? Existing loop protections. Fine.

Now, let me consider **whether `retry` outside a catch but inside a try body (not catch) should error**: e.g., `try { retry() } catch { }`. The
retry() is inside try body; vm.tries has a frame with inCatch=false. vm.retry() looks for inCatch=true, finds none → panics "retry is not allowed
outside a catch block". This panic occurs inside try body, so it's caught by the catch! So `try { retry() } catch e { errtype(e) }` would return
"custom" (or "retry"? message contains "retry" → my classifier returns "retry"). Let me verify.

block". My current classifier checks `strings.Contains(msg, "retry")` which matches both. Let me change to `strings.Contains(msg, "retry limit")` or
`"retry exhausted"` to be precise. But `ErrRetryExhausted` message is "retry limit exceeded". Let me use `"retry limit"` and `"retry exhausted"`.

+func (vm *VM) push(value any) {

+func (vm *VM) current() any {

+func (vm *VM) pop() any {
+	if len(vm.Stack) == 0 {
+		panic("stack underflow")
+	}
+	value := vm.Stack[len(vm.Stack)-1]
+	vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:len(vm.Stack)-1]

+func (vm *VM) scope() *Scope {
+	return vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]

+// allocScope returns a pointer to a Scope from the pool, growing the pool if needed.
+// Callers must set Len and exactly one of: Ints, Floats, Strings, Anys, or Array.
+func (vm *VM) allocScope() *Scope {
+	if vm.scopePoolIdx >= len(vm.scopePool) {
+		vm.scopePool = append(vm.scopePool, Scope{})
+	}
+	s := &vm.scopePool[vm.scopePoolIdx]
+	vm.scopePoolIdx++
+	// Reset iteration state
+	s.Index = 0
+	s.Count = 0
+	s.Acc = nil
+	// Clear typed slice pointers to avoid stale fast-path matches
+	s.Ints = nil
+	s.Floats = nil
+	s.Strings = nil
+	s.Anys = nil
+	// Clear Array to release reference for GC (only matters for fallback path)
+	s.Array = reflect.Value{}
+	return s
+}
+
+// getArgsForFunc lazily initializes the buffer the first time it is called for
+// a given program (thus, it also needs "program" to run). It will
+// take "needed" elements from the buffer and populate them with vm.pop() in
+// reverse order. Because the estimation can fall short, this function can
+// occasionally make a new allocation.
+func (vm *VM) getArgsForFunc(argsBuf []any, program *Program, needed int) (args []any, argsBufOut []any) {
+	if needed == 0 || program == nil {
+		return nil, argsBuf
+	}
+
+	// Step 1: fix estimations and preallocate
+	if argsBuf == nil {
+		estimatedFnArgsCount := estimateFnArgsCount(program)
+		if estimatedFnArgsCount > maxFnArgsBuf {
+			// put a practical limit to avoid excessive preallocation
+			estimatedFnArgsCount = maxFnArgsBuf
+		}
+		if estimatedFnArgsCount < needed {
+			// in the case that the first call is for example OpCallN with a large
+			// number of arguments, then make sure we will be able to serve them at
+			// least.
+			estimatedFnArgsCount = needed
+		}
+
+		// in the case that we are preparing the arguments for the first
+		// function call of the program, then argsBuf will be nil, so we
+		// initialize it. We delay this initial allocation here because a
+		// program could have many function calls but exit earlier than the
+		// first call, so in that case we avoid allocating unnecessarily
+		argsBuf = make([]any, estimatedFnArgsCount)
+	}
+
+	// Step 2: get the final slice that will be returned
+	var buf []any
+	if len(argsBuf) >= needed {
+		// in this case, we are successfully using the single preallocation. We
+		// use the full slice expression [low : high : max] because in that way
+		// a function that receives this slice as variadic arguments will not be
+		// able to make modifications to contiguous elements with append(). If
+		// they call append on their variadic arguments they will make a new
+		// allocation.
+		buf = (argsBuf)[:needed:needed]
+		argsBuf = (argsBuf)[needed:] // advance the buffer
+	} else {
+		// if we have been making calls to something like OpCallN with many more
+		// arguments than what we estimated, then we will need to allocate
+		// separately
+		buf = make([]any, needed)
+	}
+
+	// Step 3: populate the final slice bulk copying from the stack. This is the
+	// exact order and copy() is a highly optimized operation
+	copy(buf, vm.Stack[len(vm.Stack)-needed:])
+	vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:len(vm.Stack)-needed]
+
+	return buf, argsBuf
+}
+
+func (vm *VM) Step() {
+	vm.step <- struct{}{}
+}
+
+func (vm *VM) Position() chan int {
+	return vm.curr
+}
+
+func clearSlice[S ~[]E, E any](s S) {
+	var zero E
+	for i := range s {
+		s[i] = zero // clear mem, optimized by the compiler, in Go 1.21 the "clear" builtin can be used
+	}
+}
+
+// estimateFnArgsCount inspects a *Program and estimates how many function
+// arguments will be required to run it.
+func estimateFnArgsCount(program *Program) int {
+	// Implementation note: a program will not necessarily go through all
+	// operations, but this is just an estimation
+	var count int
+	for _, op := range program.Bytecode {
+		if int(op) < len(opArgLenEstimation) {
+			count += opArgLenEstimation[op]
+		}
+	}
+	return count
+}
+
+var opArgLenEstimation = [...]int{
+	OpCall1: 1,
+	OpCall2: 2,
+	OpCall3: 3,
+	// we don't know exactly but we know at least 4, so be conservative as this
+	// is only an optimization and we also want to avoid excessive preallocation
+	OpCallN: 4,
+	// here we don't know either, but we can guess it could be common to receive
+	// up to 3 arguments in a function
+	OpCallFast: 3,
+	OpCallSafe: 3,
+}
+

Now, let me think about one potential issue: I changed `getArgsForFunc` to be called with `vm.fnArgsBuf`. But `getArgsForFunc` returns `argsBufOut`
which we assign back to `vm.fnArgsBuf`. That's fine.

Now, there's a subtle issue with **`fnArgsBuf` and retry**: When a function call inside a try panics (e.g., `throw`), the `fnArgsBuf` has advanced. On
retry, `vm.fnArgsBuf` continues from the advanced position. `getArgsForFunc` handles `len(argsBuf) < needed` by allocating a new buffer. So
correctness is maintained. Good.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

type S struct{ Foo int }

func main() {
	count := 0
	env := map[string]any{
		"a": []int{1, 2, 3},
		"s": (*S)(nil),
		"fail": func() any {
			count++
			if count < 3 { panic("boom") }
			return count
		},
	}
	run := func(name, input string) {
		out, err := expr.Eval(input, env)
		fmt.Printf("%-32s => %#v, %v\n", name, out, err)
	}

	run("try fn success", `try(1+2, 99)`)
	run("try fn fallback", `try(a[10], 99)`)
	run("try block", `try { a[10] } catch { 42 }`)
	run("named catch", `try { a[10] } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	run("substr catch", `try { a[10] } catch e is "out of range" { errtype(e) }`)
	run("finally", `try { a[10] } catch { 42 } finally { 7 }`)
	run("throw", `try { throw("boom") } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	run("retry", `try { fail() } catch e { retry() }`)
	run("errtype none", `errtype(nil)`)
	run("nil deref", `try { s.Foo } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	run("in expr", `1 + try(a[10], 41)`)
	run("lazy", `try(1, throw("no"))`)
}
EOF

Add try/catch/finally expressions with runtime error handling:

- try(expr, fallback) function form with lazy fallback evaluation
- try { ... } catch { ... } block form
- optional catch <name> { ... } to bind the error
- catch <name> is \"substring\" { ... } to filter errors by message

try blocks, restoring stack, scopes and variables before entering the

Everything is committed and tests pass. Let me do one last review of the requirements to make sure I haven't missed anything:

- `try(expression, fallback)` - returns expression result on success or lazily-evaluated fallback on error; requires exactly two arguments. ✅
- `try { expr } catch { handler }` - block form; optionally `catch <name> { ... }` to bind the error. ✅
- `catch <name> is "substring" { ... }` - catches only errors whose message contains the substring. ✅
- `finally { cleanup }` - optional clause that always executes after try/catch; if finally body throws, that error propagates (overriding prior
result). ✅ (even on catch-throws and filter-mismatch)
- `throw(value)` - throws a custom error from any value (message is string conversion); requires exactly one argument. ✅
- `retry` - usable inside catch blocks, re-executes try body; limit three retries before distinct exhaustion error. Using retry outside a catch block
raises a runtime error. ✅
- `errtype(err)` - classifies caught error; requires exactly one arg. Returns:
  - "index" for out-of-range/bounds ✅
  - "conversion" for type-conversion failures ✅
  - "type" for type-mismatch/assertion errors ✅
  - "nil" for nil-pointer/reference errors ✅
  - "retry" for retry-exhaustion errors ✅
  - "custom" for all other errors including throw ✅
  - "none" when input is nil ✅

STEP 203 SOURCE agent
MESSAGE
I have completed the implementation. Here's a summary of what was done:

## Summary

Added comprehensive error handling to the expr language on a new branch `feat/error-handling` from `master`.

### New language features
- **`try(expression, fallback)`** — function form with lazy fallback evaluation (requires exactly 2 args)
- **`try { expr } catch { handler }`** — block form
- **`catch <name> { ... }`** — binds the caught error to a variable
- **`catch <name> is "substring" { ... }`** — catches only errors whose message contains the substring
- **`finally { cleanup }`** — always executes after try/catch (including when catch throws or filter doesn't match); if it throws, that error
overrides the prior result
- **`throw(value)`** — throws a custom error whose message is the string conversion of the value (requires exactly 1 arg)
- **`retry()`** — usable inside catch blocks to re-execute the try body, with a 3-retry limit and a distinct exhaustion error; raises a runtime error
outside a catch
- **`errtype(err)`** — classifies caught errors into `"index"`, `"conversion"`, `"type"`, `"nil"`, `"retry"`, `"custom"`, or `"none"` for nil

### Implementation highlights
- New AST node (`TryNode`), parser/lexer support (`try`, `catch`, `finally` keywords), checker support, compiler support
- New VM opcodes (`OpTry`, `OpEndTry`, `OpEndCatch`, `OpRetry`, `OpEndFinally`) with a try-frame stack that restores stack/scopes/variables on error
- Runtime errors are now catchable inside the VM; panics are converted to error values
- New builtins registered (`throw`, `retry`, `errtype`)
- Added `vm/runtime/errors.go` with `RuntimeError`, retry-exhaustion error, and panic-to-error conversion
- Updated `runtime.Fetch`/`FetchField`/`FetchMethod` to produce nil-specific error messages for correct `errtype` classification
- Added comprehensive tests in `error_handling_test.go` and documentation

All existing tests pass (`go test ./...`), and the changes are committed.
